Perturbative renormalization of the 𝑻 ​ 𝑻 ¯ \displaystyle T\bar{T} -deformed free massive Dirac fermion
Anshuman Dey, Aryeh Fortinsky

Abstract
In this paper we explicitly carry out the perturbative renormalization of the T ​ T ¯ \displaystyle T\bar{T} -deformed free massive Dirac fermion in two dimensions up to second order in the coupling constant. This is done by computing the two-to-two S \displaystyle S -matrix using the LSZ reduction formula and canceling out the divergences by introducing counterterms. We demonstrate that the renormalized Lagrangian is unambiguously determined by demanding that it gives the correct S \displaystyle S -matrix of a T ​ T ¯ \displaystyle T\bar{T} -deformed integrable field theory. Remarkably, the renormalized Lagrangian is qualitatively very different from its classical counterpart.



1
Introduction

Quantum field theory (QFT) is one of the most elegant and accurate frameworks which describes nature to a very high accuracy. QFTs admit deformations by operators which trigger a flow, known as the renormalization group (RG) flow, as we look into the theory at different scales
Wilson
,
Polchinski
. The fixed points of the RG-flow trajectory define conformal field theories (CFT) which are crucial in understanding aspects of condensed matter physics and string theory. However, it is equally significant to understand the RG flow away from the fixed points by deforming the QFT with a relevant or irrelevant operator. A relevant deformation stimulates the flow at lower energies (IR) while an irrelevant deformation drives the flow at higher energies (UV). Exploring the later is more difficult as it requires infinitely many counterterms in computing physical quantities in such a theory. However, a special kind of irrelevant deformation, known as the the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation, has been introduced recently in two dimensions
Zamolodchikov
,
Smirnov
and has received wide attention in the past few years as the theory is solvable.

The
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation is generated by the irrelevant operator
\displaystyle\det(T_{\mu\nu})
, the determinant of the energy-momentum tensor
\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}
of the theory, and thus can be considered in any generic QFT. At the classical level, the Lagrangian of the deformed theory itself looks very interesting and was obtained in
Andrea
,
Bonelli_2018
. For example, the Lagrangian of a
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massless boson is equivalent to the Nambu-Goto action for a string in three spacetime dimensions in the static gauge
Andrea
. Although the deformation makes the theory non-local, often complicated and non-renormalizable, there are several remarkable features that make the deformed theory so compelling. One such feature is that the energy spectrum of the deformed theory can be derived non-perturbatively and in a compact form
Zamolodchikov
. However, the spectrum appears to be sensitive to the sign of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling. For one of the signs, the highly excited states in the spectrum carry complex energies where the deformed theory becomes non-unitary. For this particular sign of the coupling, a holographic dual was proposed in
McGough
and was further investigated in
Kraus
-
Caputa
. For the other sign of the coupling, the spectrum is real and the deformed theory is unitary. Moreover, at high energies, the density of states exhibit Hagedorn behaviour like the two dimensional little string theory (LST). In the context of holography, it was argued that certain single-trace
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation of two dimensional CFTs corresponds to a two dimensional vacuum of LST
Giveon1
, see
Giveon2
-
Barbon
for related exciting works in this direction.
Another interesting aspect of the two dimensional
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed theories is the partition function - one can compute the partition function of the deformed theory on a torus or a cylinder or a disk and each of them satisfy a linear diffusion-type differential equation
Cardy1
. In particular,
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation of a CFT was considered in
Datta
and the torus partition function of the deformed CFT was obtained to be modular invariant despite the fact that the deformed theory is not conformal. See
Aharony
,
Jiang
for further exciting results concerning the partition functions in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed CFTs.

Although the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation can be defined in any two dimensional QFT, a special interest has been taken in the study of integrable quantum systems
Smirnov
,
Delfino
-
Caselle
. Integrable QFTs contain infinite number of conserved charges. The reason for this interest is that a
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
deformation preserves the integrable structure of the theory
Smirnov
. In
Smirnov
, it was also argued that the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation modifies the
\displaystyle S
-matrix only by a CDD phase factor.

Being an irrelevant deformation, the Lagrangian of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed theory is apparently non-renormalizable as it involves an infinite number of counterterms. However, it is the integrable structure of the theory that enables one to obtain a renormalized Lagrangian in such a naively non-renormalizable theory. The
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed integrable theory provides an infinite number of constraints which uniquely fix the all counterterms that appear in the theory. In
Rosenhaus
, the authors considered a
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free scalar field theory and showed how to derive the renormalized Lagrangian perturbatively by demanding that it should produce the correct
\displaystyle S
-matrix. Obtaining a renormalized Lagrangian unambiguously allows one to compute all physical quantities of interest. This motivates us to consider the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion in two dimensions and to compute the renormalized Lagrangian of such a theory perturbatively. As we will see, the renormalized Lagrangian exists and is qualitatively very different from the classical Lagrangian. In particular, while the classical Lagrangian has only one scale, namely the bare coupling constant, the renormalized Lagrangian to second order reveals three different scales in the theory.

In this paper, the perturbative renormalization of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion in two dimensions will be explicitly performed. This will be done using the LSZ reduction formula to compute the two-to-two
\displaystyle S
-matrix and introducing counterterms to cancel divergences. The paper is organized as follows: In section
2
the basics of
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformations as well as some related aspects of integrability will be reviewed. In section
3
the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion in two dimensions will be considered. In section
4
the renormalized Lagrangian to second order in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling constant will be computed. Finally, in section
5
the main results and future directions will be discussed.

2
Integrability and
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformations

In this section several aspects of integrability, two-dimensional quantum field theories and
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformations are discussed.

In two dimensional integrable theories the
\displaystyle S
-matrix of any scattering process factorizes into two-to-two
\displaystyle S
-matrices and there is no particle production
ZZ
-
Bombardelli
.
Consider a two-to-two scattering process of particles with identical mass
\displaystyle m
in two dimensions. The 2-momentum of the
\displaystyle i^{th}
particle can be parameterized by its rapidity,
\displaystyle\theta_{i}
1
1

1

Rapidity or the parameter of velocity is defined as,
\displaystyle\theta_{i}=\tanh^{-1}(v_{i})
, where
\displaystyle v_{i}
is the velocity of the
\displaystyle i
-th particle and the speed of light is set to
\displaystyle c=1
.
, as,

p_{i}^{\mu}=\left(\begin{array}[]{lr}m\cosh\theta_{i}\\
m\sinh\theta_{i}\end{array}\right)\ .

(1)

Identify
\displaystyle p_{i}^{0}=m\cosh\theta_{i}\equiv E_{i}(\theta_{i})
as the energy of the
\displaystyle i^{th}
particle and
\displaystyle p_{i}^{1}=m\sinh\theta_{i}\equiv P_{i}(\theta_{i})
as the momentum of the
\displaystyle i^{th}
particle.

Two dimensional kinematics of the two-to-two scattering processes have the unique property that the incoming momenta of the particles equal the outgoing momenta of the particles. This fact and the Lorentz invariance implies that the
\displaystyle S
-matrix, denoted by
\displaystyle S(\theta)
, will only be a function of the difference of rapidities,
\displaystyle\theta=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}
.

In what follows the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation of an integrable field theory will be considered. If a
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation is performed on an integrable field theory, the deformed theory is integrable as well
Smirnov
. As we mentioned earlier, there is no particle production in a scattering process in an integrable theory and hence, unitarity demands

\displaystyle\displaystyle|S(\theta)|^{2}=1\ .

(2)

On the other hand, the crossing symmetry of the
\displaystyle S
-matrix
2
2

2

It is the symmetry of the
\displaystyle S
-matrix under the interchange of the
\displaystyle s
and
\displaystyle u
-channels as will be shown later.
implies,

\displaystyle\displaystyle S(\theta)=S(i\pi-\theta)\ .

(3)

The solution to (
2
) and (
3
) is simple and given by the CDD factor,

\displaystyle\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(\theta)=\frac{\sinh\theta-i\sin\alpha}{\sinh\theta+i\sin\alpha}\ ,

(4)

where
\displaystyle\alpha
is a real parameter related to the coupling constant. The product of
\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(\theta)
over
\displaystyle\alpha
,
\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(\theta)
, is also a solution to (
2
) and (
3
).

An alternative solution for the CDD factor was recently considered by Smirnov and Zamolodchikov
Smirnov
, which admits the following representation

\displaystyle\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(\theta)=e^{i\alpha\sinh\theta}\ .

(5)

The authors considered
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed theories in two dimensions
3
3

3

To be precise, the authors in
Smirnov
considered integrable quantum field theories (IQFT) and deformed them by generic scalars
\displaystyle X_{s}
such that the deformations preserve integrability. Being IQFT, these theories have an infinite number of conserved currents (
\displaystyle T_{s+1}(z),\Theta_{s-1}(z)
) and (
\displaystyle\bar{T}_{s+1}(z),\bar{\Theta}_{s-1}(z)
), where the index
\displaystyle s
represents the spin of the corresponding fields thus labeling the currents. The scalars
\displaystyle X_{s}
are defined in terms of these local currents. For example,
\displaystyle X_{1}
is precisely the composite operator
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
considered in our paper.
where the deformation produces a one-parameter family of Lagrangians obeying the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
flow equation,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}=-4\Big(T^{\lambda}(z)\bar{T}^{\lambda}(z)-\theta^{\lambda}(z)\bar{\theta}^{\lambda}(z)\Big)\ ,

(6)

\displaystyle\lambda
being the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling constant,
\displaystyle T^{\lambda}=T_{zz}^{\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\bar{T}^{\lambda}=T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}^{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle\theta^{\lambda}=\bar{\theta}^{\lambda}=T_{z\bar{z}}^{\lambda}
are the components of the energy momentum tensor of the deformed theory and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda=0)
is the Lagrangian of the undeformed theory.
It was argued that the deformed theory is integrable and the
\displaystyle S
-matrix of this theory can be expressed in a factorizable form,
\displaystyle\hat{S^{\prime}}(\theta)S(\theta)
, where
\displaystyle S(\theta)
is the CDD factor determined by unitarity and crossing-symmetry of the
\displaystyle S
-matrix,

\displaystyle\displaystyle S(\theta)=e^{i\lambda m^{2}\sinh\theta}\ .

(7)

The factor
\displaystyle\hat{S^{\prime}}(\theta)
signifies the presence of mass degeneracies in the spectrum and it satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation
Bombardelli
. This typically fixes the “flavor” structure of the
\displaystyle S
-matrix. Simply, the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformation (
6
) corresponds to multiplying the
\displaystyle S
-matrix by the factor (
7
).

However, observe that (
7
) grows exponentially at large imaginary momenta.
This behaviour is inconsistent with the analytic behaviour of
\displaystyle S
-matrices in a local QFT.
Nevertheless, rather than just throwing out these theories, one can try to understand such theories as QFTs coupled to gravity
Gorbenko
-
Conti2
.
In this paper, however, we will be restricted to low-energy regime of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed theories. In this regime these are quantum field theories and can be studied perturbatively in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling
\displaystyle\lambda
around
\displaystyle\lambda=0

Kraus
,
Rosenhaus
,
He1
-
Dey

4
4

4

For non-perturbative studies of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed theories, see
Cardy2
,
Haruna
.
.

3
The
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion

Consider the Euclidean action for the free massive Dirac fermion with mass
\displaystyle m
,

I_{0}=\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\mathcal{L}_{0}=\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\big(i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)~.

(8)

Performing a Wick rotation to the Euclidean action
\displaystyle x_{1}=it
and
\displaystyle x_{2}=x
, yields the Lorentzian action,

-I_{0}=-\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\mathcal{L}_{0}=i\int dtdx\,\big(-i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi+m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)\ ,

(9)

where the
\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}
s are the two dimensional gamma matrices satisfying the Clifford algebra,

\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2\ \eta^{\mu\nu}\ \mathbb{I}

(10)

and
\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}
is the two dimensional Minkowski metric. We represent the gamma matrices in terms of the Pauli matrices:
\displaystyle\gamma_{0}=\sigma_{x}
and
\displaystyle\gamma_{1}=-i\sigma_{y}
.

The equation of motion for the fermionic fields
\displaystyle\psi
and
\displaystyle\bar{\psi}
are given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-m\psi=0\>\>\>\>\>\>\text{and}\>\>\>\>\>\>i{\partial}_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}+m\bar{\psi}=0.

(11)

To solve the Dirac equation (
11
), make the ansatz,
\displaystyle\psi(x)=u(k)e^{-ik.x}
. Plugging the ansatz
into the Dirac equation and using the normalization
\displaystyle u^{\dagger}(k)u(k)=1
, one finds the normalized positive energy plane wave solution,

\displaystyle\displaystyle u(k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{E_{+}+k^{1}}\\
\frac{m}{\sqrt{E_{+}+k^{1}}}\end{array}\right)\hskip 8.53581pt=\hskip 5.69054pt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{k^{0}+k^{1}}\\
\sqrt{k^{0}-k^{1}}\end{array}\right)

where,
\displaystyle E_{+}=k^{0}=\sqrt{(k^{1})^{2}+m^{2}}
.

Similarly, using
\displaystyle\psi(x)=v(k)e^{ik.x}
one can obtain the negative energy plane wave solution,

\displaystyle\displaystyle v(k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\sqrt{E_{-}+k^{1}}\\
\frac{m}{\sqrt{E_{-}+k^{1}}}\end{array}\right)\hskip 8.53581pt=\hskip 5.69054pt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\sqrt{k^{0}+k^{1}}\\
\sqrt{k^{0}-k^{1}}\end{array}\right)

where,
\displaystyle E_{-}=k^{0}=-\sqrt{(k^{1})^{2}+m^{2}}
.

Hence, the solution to the wave equation for the free massive Dirac fermion is,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d\vec{k}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E}}\left(a(\vec{k})u(\vec{k})e^{-ik\cdot x}+b^{\dagger}(\vec{k})v(\vec{k})e^{ik\cdot x}\right)\ ,

(20)

where
\displaystyle a^{\dagger}(\vec{k})
and
\displaystyle a(\vec{k})
are the fermion creation and annihilation operators respectively, while
\displaystyle b^{\dagger}(\vec{k})
and
\displaystyle b(\vec{k})
are the anti-fermion creation and annihilation operators respectively. The creation and annihilation operators satisfy the Clifford algebra,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{a(\vec{k_{1}}),a^{\dagger}(\vec{k_{2}})\}=2\pi\delta(\vec{k_{1}}-\vec{k_{2}})\>\>\>\>\>\>\text{and}\>\>\>\>\>\>\{b(\vec{k_{1}}),b^{\dagger}(\vec{k_{2}})\}=2\pi\delta(\vec{k_{1}}-\vec{k_{2}})\ ,

(21)

while all other anti-commutators vanish. The Dirac adjoint of a spinor
\displaystyle\psi
is defined as,
\displaystyle\bar{\psi}=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
.

Consider the
\displaystyle\bar{T}T
-deformation of the free massive Dirac fermion in two dimensional Euclidean spacetime,

I=\int d^{\,2}x\,\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\big(i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)+\lambda\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\mathcal{O}_{T\bar{T}}\ ,

(22)

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T\bar{T}}
is the local
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-operator given by the determinant of the energy-momentum tensor,

\mathcal{O}_{T\bar{T}}=\det(T^{(\lambda)})=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\epsilon^{\rho\sigma}T_{\mu\nu}^{(\lambda)}T_{\rho\sigma}^{(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\big(T_{\mu}^{\ \mu(\lambda)}\big)^{2}-T_{\mu\nu}^{(\lambda)}T^{\mu\nu(\lambda)}\Big]\ ,

(23)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu}
the two-dimensional Levi-Civita tensor and
\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}^{(\lambda)}
the energy-momentum tensor of the finite-
\displaystyle\lambda
theory.

The canonical energy-momentum tensor of the undeformed theory is given by,

T_{\mu\nu(c)}^{(0)}=X_{\mu\nu}-\delta_{\mu\nu}(\Tr X-m\bar{\psi}\psi)\ ,

(24)

where

\displaystyle\displaystyle X_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{2}\big(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}{\partial}_{\nu}\psi-{\partial}_{\nu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi\big)\ .

(25)

The above canonical energy-momentum tensor can be symmetrized using the Belinfante technique, yielding

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}-\delta_{\mu\nu}(\Tr X-m\bar{\psi}\psi)\ ,

(26)

where

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\Big(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi-{\partial}_{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu)}\psi\Big)

(27)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{i}{4}\big(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}{\partial}_{\nu}\psi+\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-{\partial}_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu}\psi-{\partial}_{\nu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi\big)\ .

The
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed Lagrangian of the free massive Dirac fermion can be obtained by solving the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
flow equation
Bonelli_2018
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}(\lambda)}{\partial{\lambda}}=\mathcal{O}_{T\bar{T}}\ ,

(28)

with the initial condition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda=0)=\mathcal{L}_{0}
.

Solving (
28
) perturbatively in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling
\displaystyle\lambda
, one finds the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed Lagrangian for the free massive Dirac fermion
Bonelli_2018
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)-\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}+2m\bar{\psi}\psi\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}-2m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\Big)

(29)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}
is given by (
27
). It is noteworthy to mention that the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed Lagrangian (
29
) is exact in
\displaystyle\lambda
, all the higher order terms in
\displaystyle\lambda
vanish identically due to the Grassmann nature of the fermion fields.
5
5

5

Although, terms proportional to
\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{4}
can be present at third order in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling
\displaystyle\lambda
, the authors of
Bonelli_2018
claimed that the
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})
term vanishes by using Fierz identities. It is also possible to verify this claim directly by plugging the expression into MATHEMATICA.

Therefore, the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed Lorentzian action of the free massive Dirac fermion is,

\displaystyle\displaystyle-I=-\int dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\mathcal{L}=i

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle\displaystyle dtdx\,\Big[\big(-i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi+m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle 2m\bar{\psi}\psi\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}-2m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\Big)-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)\Big]

In what follows two-to-two scattering in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion theory will be considered. The
\displaystyle S
-matrix,
\displaystyle S(\theta)
, will be computed perturbatively to second order in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling
\displaystyle\lambda
. Finally the
\displaystyle S
-matrix will be compared with (
7
) and the renormalized Lagrangian will be constructed.

4
Renormalization of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion

In section
3
, the classical Lagrangian of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion was stated. In this section the renormalized Lagrangian will be computed to second order.

In order to compute the renormalized Lagrangian similar methods to those found in
Rosenhaus
will be used. First, the
\displaystyle S
-matrix will be computed using the classical Lagrangian giving
rise to UV divergences. Next, counterterms will be added to the Lagrangian in order to
cancel the divergences and ensure that the final
\displaystyle S
-matrix is given by (
7
).

4.1
The
\displaystyle S
-matrix

Consider the two-to-two scattering of a fermion and anti-fermion in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac theory
6
6

6

One can also consider the other possible two-to-two scattering, namely, the fermion-fermion scattering. However, the resulting renormalized Lagrangian would be the same as it does not depend on the particular scattering process. Because of this fact only the fermion anti-fermion scattering process is considered.
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle f_{1}+\bar{f_{2}}\rightarrow f_{3}+\bar{f_{4}}

where
\displaystyle f_{1}
represents the incoming fermion with momentum
\displaystyle p_{1}
,
\displaystyle\bar{f_{2}}
the incoming anti-fermion with momentum
\displaystyle p_{2}
,
\displaystyle f_{3}
the outgoing fermion with momentum
\displaystyle p_{3}
and
\displaystyle\bar{f_{4}}
the outgoing anti-fermion with momentum
\displaystyle p_{4}
.

Recall, due to the two dimensional kinematics of two-to-two scattering the momenta of the incoming particles equal the momenta of the outgoing particles. When considering fermion anti-fermion scattering the
\displaystyle 0^{th}
-order
\displaystyle S
-matrix is just the identity. Hence, the momentum of the incoming fermion must equal the momentum of the outgoing fermion and the momentum of the incoming anti-fermion must equal the momentum of the outgoing anti-fermion
7
7

7

If scattering in a scalar theory is considered
Rosenhaus
, one can choose
\displaystyle p_{1}=p_{4}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}=p_{3}
also. However, it is easy to see that if one works with fermions, we must have
\displaystyle p_{1}=p_{3}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}=p_{4}
to have a non-zero scattering matrix.
. In particular,

\displaystyle\displaystyle p_{1}=p_{3}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ p_{2}=p_{4}.

(31)

By (
1
) and (
31
) the Mandelstam variables in the
\displaystyle(+,-)
signature take the form,

\displaystyle\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=2m^{2}(1+\cosh\theta)\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle(p_{1}-p_{3})^{2}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2}=2m^{2}(1-\cosh\theta)=4m^{2}-s\ .

(32)

Observe that the Mandelstam variables can be related to each other by the transformations,

\displaystyle\displaystyle u=s|_{\theta\rightarrow i\pi-\theta}\ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ t=s|_{\theta\rightarrow i\pi}.

(33)

Finally due to (
31
), for the two-to-two scattering in an integrable theory the
\displaystyle S
-matrix,
\displaystyle S(\theta)
, is defined as,

\displaystyle\displaystyle{}_{out}\langle p_{3},p_{4}|p_{1},p_{2}\rangle_{in}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\delta(p_{1}-p_{3})\delta(p_{2}-p_{4})2E(p_{1})2E(p_{2})S(\theta)\ ,

(34)

where the zeroth order
\displaystyle S
-matrix in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling is
\displaystyle S^{(0)}(\theta)=1
.
The
\displaystyle S
-matrix
\displaystyle S(\theta)
is related to the scattering amplitude
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}
by
Rosenhaus
,

S(\theta)=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{4m^{2}\sinh\theta}~.

(35)

However, before computing the
\displaystyle S
-matrix, express the deformed action (
) in a simpler form. By the field redefinitions,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\psi_{a}\rightarrow\psi_{a}+\lambda\frac{m}{2}\psi_{a}(\bar{\psi}\psi)\>\>\>\>\>\text{and}\>\>\>\>\>\bar{\psi}_{a}\rightarrow\bar{\psi}_{a}+\lambda\frac{m}{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)\bar{\psi}_{a}\ ,

(36)

where
\displaystyle a=\{1,2\}
are the spinor indices, one can write the action (
) as,

\displaystyle\displaystyle-I=i\int dtdx\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big[

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(-i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi+m\bar{\psi}\psi\big)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\mu})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\mu})^{2}\Big)+\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\ \tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\mu}-\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{3}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}\Big].

For details see Appendix
A
.

Observe that the new action no longer contains linear terms of the form
\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\psi\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}
and
\displaystyle(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}
, making it simpler to compute the one-loop bubble diagrams by decreasing the total number of terms. But, at second order, two new terms
\displaystyle(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}
and
\displaystyle(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}
are generated which contribute to the tadpole diagrams at one-loop. However, these new terms give vanishing contributions as will be shown later.

The two dimensional Dirac spinors
\displaystyle u(p_{i})
,
\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{i})
,
\displaystyle v(p_{i})
and
\displaystyle\bar{v}(p_{i})
will arise in the computation of the
\displaystyle S
-matrix. Using (
3
), (
3
) and the fact that
\displaystyle p_{3}^{\mu}=p_{1}^{\mu}=(m\cosh\theta_{1},\ m\sinh\theta_{1})
and
\displaystyle p_{4}^{\mu}=p_{2}^{\mu}=(m\cosh\theta_{2},\ m\sinh\theta_{2})
the two dimensional Dirac spinors can be written as,

\displaystyle\displaystyle u(p_{1})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}+\sinh\theta_{1}}\\
\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}-\sinh\theta_{1}}\end{array}\right)=u(p_{3})\ ,\hskip 11.38109ptu(p_{2})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}+\sinh\theta_{2}}\\
\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}-\sinh\theta_{2}}\end{array}\right)=u(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle v(p_{1})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}+\sinh\theta_{1}}\\
\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}-\sinh\theta_{1}}\end{array}\right)=v(p_{3})\ ,\hskip 11.38109ptv(p_{2})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}+\sinh\theta_{2}}\\
\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}-\sinh\theta_{2}}\end{array}\right)=v(p_{4})\ .

The Dirac adjoints
\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{i})=u(p_{i})^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
and
\displaystyle\bar{v}(p_{i})=v(p_{i})^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
can be written as,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{1})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}-\sinh\theta_{1}}\hskip 28.45274pt\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}+\sinh\theta_{1}}\end{array}\right)=\bar{u}(p_{3})\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}(p_{2})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}-\sinh\theta_{2}}\hskip 28.45274pt\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}+\sinh\theta_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\bar{u}(p_{4})\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}(p_{1})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}-\sinh\theta_{1}}\hskip 28.45274pt-\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{1}+\sinh\theta_{1}}\end{array}\right)=\bar{v}(p_{3})\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}(p_{2})=\sqrt{m}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}-\sinh\theta_{2}}\hskip 28.45274pt-\sqrt{\cosh\theta_{2}+\sinh\theta_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\bar{v}(p_{4})\ .

It is finally time to compute the
\displaystyle S
-matrix perturbatively up to second order in the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-coupling
\displaystyle\lambda
using the redefined Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}_{0}+\mathcal{L}_{1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{L}_{2}(\lambda)\ ,

(51)

where,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi+m\bar{\psi}\psi\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2}\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)+\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}-\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{3}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}.

(52)

4.1.2
Second order
\displaystyle S
-matrix

At second order the
\displaystyle S
-matrix gets contribution from one-loop diagrams. At one-loop, two types of diagrams can contribute to the second order amplitude: the first one is the tadpole diagram while the second one is the bubble diagram. Both of these diagrams will be computed in this section.

Contribution from tadpole diagrams

The second order Lagrangian of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermion can be written as,

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\mathcal{L}_{2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\Big[\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)+\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}-\frac{7}{4}\lambda^{2}m^{3}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}\Big]

(65)

where,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{\psi}\psi\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma^{(\mu}{\partial}^{\nu)}\psi-2\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi+{\partial}_{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\bar{\psi}\psi(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi\ \bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu}{\partial}_{\nu}\psi-2\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi\ {\partial}_{\nu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu}\psi+{\partial}_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\ {\partial}_{\nu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu}\psi\Big)\ ,

(67)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip-202.01474pt(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\big(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-{\partial}_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\big)\ .

(68)

Since the interaction vertices contain six fields, two internal fields must be contracted resulting in loops.

Figure 2:
Tadpole diagram that contributes to the
\displaystyle S
-matrix at second order.

The sextic couplings give rise to the one-loop tadpole diagrams shown in figure
2
and contribute to the second order
\displaystyle S
-matrix. The corresponding amplitude is given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{tad}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[m\bar{\psi}\psi\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)+\frac{7}{2}m^{2}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{2}\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu}

(69)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 122.34692pt-\frac{7}{2}m^{3}(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle.

Just as in the first order case one can evaluate (
69
) by first deriving the general amplitude of a general sextic coupling,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}\bar{\psi}_{e}\psi_{f}}^{\text{tad}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}\bar{\psi}_{e}\psi_{f}\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\left\langle\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\left\langle\psi_{d}(x)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\left\langle\psi_{f}(x)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\left\langle\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\left\langle\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{e}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\left\langle\psi_{d}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\left\langle\psi_{f}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\left\langle\psi_{d}(x)\bar{\psi}_{e}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{f}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{f}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\left\langle\psi_{f}(x)\bar{\psi}_{e}(x)\right\rangle\Big[\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})+\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})v_{d}(p_{4})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})-\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})v_{d}(p_{4})\Big]

where all possible Wick contractions of fermionic fields were performed. The internal propagators give rise to loops whose values are derived in Appendix
B
and given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle\psi_{a}(x)\bar{\psi}_{b}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle\displaystyle=N_{0}\delta_{ab},

(71)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle\partial^{\mu}\psi_{a}(x)\bar{\psi}_{b}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\langle\psi_{a}(x)\partial^{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{b}(x)\rangle=N_{1}\gamma^{\mu}_{ab},

(72)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle\partial^{\mu}\psi_{a}(x)\partial^{\nu}\bar{\psi}_{b}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle\displaystyle=imN_{1}\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{ab}\,

(73)

where,

\displaystyle\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{m}{4\pi}\log(\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}})\ ,

(74)

\displaystyle\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{i\Lambda^{2}}{8\pi}\Big[1+\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\log(\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}})\Big]\ ,

(75)

and
\displaystyle\Lambda
is the UV cut-off.

), one can compute the contribution of
\displaystyle(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}
to the amplitude,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\big[(\bar{\psi}\psi)^{3}\big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle

(76)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}\delta_{ef}\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\big[\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}\bar{\psi}_{e}\psi_{f}\big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle=0

where (

), (
) and (

) were used to obtain the above result.

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\bar{v}(p_{2})\cdot v(p_{4})\ \bar{u}(p_{3})\cdot(-i)\gamma^{(\mu}p_{1}^{\nu)}\cdot u(p_{1})-\bar{u}(p_{3})\cdot u(p_{1})\ \bar{v}(p_{2})\cdot i\gamma^{(\mu}p_{4}^{\nu)}\cdot v(p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\bar{v}(p_{2})\cdot v(p_{4})\ \bar{u}(p_{3})\cdot(-i)\gamma_{(\mu}p_{1\nu)}\cdot u(p_{1})-\bar{u}(p_{3})\cdot u(p_{1})\ \bar{v}(p_{2})\cdot i\gamma_{(\mu}p_{4\nu)}\cdot v(p_{4})\Big]\,

Notice that the last term in (

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}\psi{\partial}_{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi}^{\text{tad}}=\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}\psi{\partial}_{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle=0.

(79)

The second term in (
) gives a non-zero contribution to the amplitude and can be computed in a similar way,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi}^{\text{tad}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{m^{3}\Lambda^{2}}{\pi}\Big[1+4\big(2-\cosh\theta\big)\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\log\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big]\cosh^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}.

), (

) and (
80
) according to (

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\ \frac{1}{4}\Big[\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma^{(\mu}{\partial}^{\nu)}\psi}^{\text{tad}}-2\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}\psi\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\mu}{\partial}_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi}^{\text{tad}}+\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\psi}\psi{\partial}_{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu)}\psi{\partial}^{(\mu}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\nu)}\psi}^{\text{tad}}\Big]

) one can compute the contribution from the sextic coupling

\displaystyle\displaystyle 0

Similarly, evaluating the contribution to the amplitude from each term in

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{\text{tad}}(\theta)=\frac{\mathcal{A}^{\text{tad}}}{4m^{2}\sinh\theta}=-i\frac{\lambda^{2}m^{4}}{32\pi}\coth\frac{\theta}{2}\Big[3\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}+4\big(2-\cosh\theta\big)\log\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big]\ .

(86)

Contribution from bubble diagrams

The final and most complicated contribution to the
\displaystyle S
-matrix is the contribution that arises from the first order term in the Lagrangian squared,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2!}\left(i\mathcal{L}_{1}(\lambda)\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2!}\Big(\frac{i\lambda}{2}\Big)^{2}\Big(\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}-(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\mu})^{2}\Big)^{2}.

(87)

These interaction vertices give rise to one-loop bubble diagrams of the form figure
3
.

(a)

\displaystyle s
-channel

(b)

\displaystyle t
-channel

(c)

\displaystyle u
-channel

Figure 3:
The
\displaystyle s
,
\displaystyle t
and
\displaystyle u
-channels of a typical bubble diagram that contribute to the
\displaystyle S
-matrix
at second order.

From (
87
) there are three different kinds of bubble diagrams:

(a)

both vertices contain
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}
interactions,

(b)

one vertex contains interaction
\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\rho\lambda}\tilde{X}^{\rho\lambda}
while the other contains
\displaystyle(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}
and

(c)

both vertices contain
\displaystyle(\tilde{X}_{\mu}^{\ \mu})^{2}
interactions.

For the bubble diagram case the amplitudes will be split up into
\displaystyle s
,
\displaystyle t
and
\displaystyle u
-channel contributions to the amplitude, as shown in figure (
3
). As done in the previous cases, begin by writing down the general expressions for the amplitude where both vertices
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle y
contain arbitrary non-derivative quartic couplings. These expressions will be the building blocks for the computation of the amplitude from the bubble diagrams.

In order to compute the general contributions of the
\displaystyle s
,
\displaystyle t
and
\displaystyle u
-channels to the amplitude consider the case when the vertex
\displaystyle x
involves a quartic coupling
\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\psi_{d}(x)
and vertex
\displaystyle y
contains another quartic coupling
\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{e}(y)\psi_{f}(y)\bar{\psi}_{g}(y)\psi_{h}(y)
.

The general contribution of the
\displaystyle s
-channel (figure
3(a)
) to the amplitude is given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{abcdefgh}^{(s)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}_{e}(y)\psi_{f}(y)\bar{\psi}_{g}(y)\psi_{h}(y)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\psi_{d}(x)\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle^{(s)}

(88)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(\xi+q)G_{bg}(q)-\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(\xi+q)G_{bg}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(\xi+q)G_{be}(q)+\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(\xi+q)G_{be}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(\xi+q)G_{dg}(q)-\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(\xi+q)G_{dg}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(\xi+q)G_{de}(q)+\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(\xi+q)G_{de}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{a}(p_{2})u_{d}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(\xi+q)G_{bg}(q)-\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(\xi+q)G_{bg}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(\xi+q)G_{be}(q)+\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(\xi+q)G_{be}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{c}(p_{2})u_{b}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(\xi+q)G_{dg}(q)-\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(\xi+q)G_{dg}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(\xi+q)G_{de}(q)+\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(\xi+q)G_{de}(q)\Big)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\xi^{2}=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=s
and
\displaystyle G_{ab}(q)=\frac{i(\gamma\cdot q+m)_{ab}}{q^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon}
is the fermionic propagator in the free theory.

Next consider the contribution of the
\displaystyle t
-channel (figure
3(b)
) to the amplitude. Since
\displaystyle p_{1}=p_{3}
, the general
\displaystyle t
-channel contribution to the amplitude is given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{abcdefgh}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}_{e}(y)\psi_{f}(y)\bar{\psi}_{g}(y)\psi_{h}(y)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\psi_{d}(x)\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle^{(t)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(q)G_{be}(q)-\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(q)G_{be}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(q)G_{bg}(q)+\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(q)G_{bg}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(q)G_{de}(q)-\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(q)G_{de}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(q)G_{dg}(q)+\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(q)G_{dg}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{a}(p_{3})u_{d}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(q)G_{be}(q)-\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(q)G_{be}(q)

(89)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fc}(q)G_{bg}(q)+\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{hc}(q)G_{bg}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{u}_{c}(p_{3})u_{b}(p_{1})\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(q)G_{de}(q)-\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(q)G_{de}(q)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{h}(p_{4})G_{fa}(q)G_{dg}(q)+\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})v_{f}(p_{4})G_{ha}(q)G_{dg}(q)\Big)\ .

Finally consider the contribution of the
\displaystyle u
-channel (figure
3(c)
) to the amplitude. The general
\displaystyle u
-channel contribution to the amplitude is given by,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{abcdefgh}^{(u)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle 0|b(p_{4})a(p_{3})\text{T}\Big[\bar{\psi}_{e}(y)\psi_{f}(y)\bar{\psi}_{g}(y)\psi_{h}(y)\bar{\psi}_{a}(x)\psi_{b}(x)\bar{\psi}_{c}(x)\psi_{d}(x)\Big]a^{\dagger}(p_{1})b^{\dagger}(p_{2})|0\rangle^{(u)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big(u_{d}(p_{1})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})-u_{d}(p_{1})v_{b}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})-u_{b}(p_{1})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{e}(p_{3})\bar{v}_{g}(p_{2})

\displaystyle\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle u_{b}(p_{1})v_{d}(p_{4})\bar{u}_{g}(p_{3})\bar{v}_{e}(p_{2})\Big)\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\Big(G_{hc}(\zeta-q)G_{fa}(q)-G_{ha}(\zeta-q)G_{fc}(q)\Big)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\zeta^{2}=(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2}=(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}=u
as
\displaystyle p_{2}=p_{4}
.

Notice that a general
\displaystyle s
-channel amplitude (
88
) involves evaluation of the one-loop integral,

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{abcd}^{(s)}(\xi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}G_{ab}(\xi+q)G_{cd}(q)=\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{i\big(\gamma\cdot(\xi+q)+m\big)_{ab}}{(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}}\frac{i\big(\gamma\cdot q+m\big)_{cd}}{q^{2}-m^{2}}

(91)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}\gamma^{\nu}_{cd}(\xi+q)_{\mu}q_{\nu}+m\delta_{ab}\gamma^{\mu}_{cd}q_{\mu}+m\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}(\xi+q)_{\mu}\delta_{cd}+m^{2}\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}}{\big[(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}\big](q^{2}-m^{2})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-L^{(s)}(\xi)\Big(m\not{\xi}_{ab}\delta_{cd}+m^{2}\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}\Big)-\Big(\not{\xi}_{ab}\not{L}^{(s)}_{cd}(\xi)+m\delta_{ab}\not{L}^{(s)}_{cd}(\xi)+m\not{L}^{(s)}_{ab}(\xi)\delta_{cd}\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}\gamma^{\nu}_{cd}L^{(s)}_{\mu\nu}(\xi)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\ \ \ \xi^{\mu}=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{\mu}
,
\displaystyle\ \ \not{L}^{(s)}_{ab}=\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}L^{(s)}_{\mu}
,
\displaystyle\ \ \not{\xi}_{ab}=\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}\xi_{\mu}\ \
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(s)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\big[(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}=-\frac{\pi+i\theta}{4\pi m^{2}\sinh\theta}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(s)}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}}{\big[(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}=\frac{\pi+i\theta}{8\pi m^{2}\sinh\theta}\ \xi_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(s)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}q_{\nu}}{\big[(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{i}{8\pi}\bigg[1+\log\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}+\big(i\pi-\theta\big)\tanh\frac{\theta}{2}\bigg]\eta_{\mu\nu}+\frac{i}{16\pi m^{2}\cosh^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}\bigg[1+\big(i\pi-\theta\big)\coth\theta\bigg]\xi_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}\ .

The above integrals are derived in detail in Appendix
C
.

Similar yet simpler integrals are involved in the evaluation of the general contribution to the amplitude of
\displaystyle t
-channel (
89
). In the
\displaystyle t
-channel case the integrals are much simpler as both the internal propagators carry momentum
\displaystyle q
since
\displaystyle p_{1}=p_{3}
and take the form,

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{abcd}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}G_{ab}(q)G_{cd}(q)

(93)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-m^{2}\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}L^{(t)}-\Big(m\delta_{ab}\not{L}^{(t)}_{cd}+m\not{L}^{(t)}_{ab}\delta_{cd}\Big)-\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}\gamma^{\nu}_{cd}L^{(t)}_{\mu\nu}\ ,

where,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(t)}=\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ L^{(t)}_{\mu}=\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}}{\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)^{2}},\ \ \ L^{(t)}_{\mu\nu}=\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}q_{\nu}}{\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)^{2}}.

(94)

However, there is no need to explicitly compute
\displaystyle L^{(t)}
,
\displaystyle L^{(t)}_{\mu}
and
\displaystyle L^{(t)}_{\mu\nu}
as their values can easily be obtained from their
\displaystyle s
-channel counterparts (
). In section
4.1
, it was shown that the
\displaystyle t
-channel corresponds to
\displaystyle\theta\rightarrow i\pi
. Thus, one can simply substitute
\displaystyle\theta\rightarrow i\pi
and
\displaystyle\xi\rightarrow 0
into (
) to obtain the corresponding
\displaystyle t
-channel loop integrals
\displaystyle L^{(t)}
,
\displaystyle L^{(t)}_{\mu}
and
\displaystyle L^{(t)}_{\mu\nu}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(t)}=\frac{i}{4\pi m^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ L^{(t)}_{\mu}=0\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ L^{(t)}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{8\pi}\Big(1-\log\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\Big)\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(95)

In the
\displaystyle u

) one must compute the following loop-integral similar to the

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{abcd}^{(u)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}G_{ab}(\zeta-q)G_{cd}(q)

(96)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle-L^{(u)}(\zeta)\Big(m\not{\zeta}_{ab}\delta_{cd}+m^{2}\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}\Big)-\Big(\not{\zeta}_{ab}\not{L}_{cd}^{(u)}(\zeta)+m\delta_{ab}\not{L}_{cd}^{(u)}(\zeta)-m\not{L}_{ab}^{(u)}(\zeta)\delta_{cd}\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\gamma^{\mu}_{ab}\gamma^{\nu}_{cd}L_{\mu\nu}^{(u)}(\zeta)\ ,

where
\displaystyle\zeta^{\mu}=(p_{1}-p_{4})^{\mu}=(p_{1}-p_{2})^{\mu}\ \ \
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(u)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\big[(\zeta-q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(u)}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}}{\big[(\zeta-q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(u)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{q_{\mu}q_{\nu}}{\big[(\zeta-q)^{2}-m^{2}\big]\big(q^{2}-m^{2}\big)}\ .

(97)

\displaystyle t
-channel case, it was shown in section

that the
\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\theta\rightarrow i\pi-\theta
. Hence, naively one can evaluate the

and

Replacing
\displaystyle\theta

is equivalent to replacing
\displaystyle\xi^{2}

\displaystyle\zeta^{2}

However, notice that in the u-channel loop integrals (

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle\zeta-q
, while in the
\displaystyle s
-channel loop integrals (

\displaystyle\xi+q

Therefore, since the functional dependence of the integrands on
\displaystyle\zeta
is
\displaystyle\zeta-q
, an extra minus sign must be included whenever the integrand is odd in
\displaystyle q
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(u)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\frac{\theta}{4\pi m^{2}\sinh\theta}\ ,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L^{(u)}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\displaystyle i\frac{\theta}{8\pi m^{2}\sinh\theta}\zeta_{\mu}\ ,

When the vertices have derivative interactions the amplitudes involve more complicated one-loop integrals,

\displaystyle\displaystyle(I_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n}})_{abcd}(\xi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{\mu_{i}}G_{ab}(\xi+q)G_{cd}(q)\ ,

(99)

which requires the computation of integrals of the form,

\displaystyle\displaystyle L_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n+2}}=\int\frac{d^{2}q}{2\pi^{2}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{n+2}q_{\mu_{i}}}{\big[(\xi+q)^{2}-m^{2}\big](q^{2}-m^{2})}

(100)

where
\displaystyle n\in\{0,...,4\}
.
A detailed discussion on the evaluation of these one-loop integrals can be found in Appendix
C
.
At this point one can plug the one-loop integral expressions and vertices into MATHEMATICA to obtain the contribution to the amplitudes from the
\displaystyle s
,
\displaystyle t
and
\displaystyle u
-channels.

It is important, however, to mention that in the
\displaystyle S
-matrix computation for a
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed scalar
Rosenhaus
, one does not need to calculate amplitudes for all the channels explicitly to compute the total amplitude. Rather, one can use the following trick: evaluate the s-channel amplitude explicitly, then consider the appropriate limits to obtain the
\displaystyle t
and
\displaystyle u
-channel amplitudes. However, such a procedure can not be performed when there are non-trivial polarization vectors that depend on the momenta or rapidities of the external particles.
To compute the total amplitude of the
\displaystyle T\bar{T}
-deformed free massive Dirac fermionic theory one must take into account the external polarization vectors described by the Dirac spinors
\displaystyle u_{a}
,
\displaystyle v_{a}
,
\displaystyle\bar{u}_{a}
and
\displaystyle\bar{v}_{a}
which depend on the rapidities
\displaystyle\theta_{i}
of the particles. Thus the naive substitution
\displaystyle\theta\rightarrow i\pi
or
\displaystyle\theta\rightarrow i\pi-\theta
into the final
\displaystyle s
-channel amplitude would produce incorrect results for the amplitude from the other channels and hence they must be explicitly computed.

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{X}^{\mu\nu}
:
